# Matters.AI Lands India’s Largest Cybersecurity Pre-Series A Round to Fight AI Data Risks

The security startup consolidates four legacy security tools into an autonomous platform that uses context-aware AI to trace sensitive data lineage, analyze user intent, and automatically remediate threats across enterprise environments.

November 27, 2026

2 Min Read

![Image 7](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte7893676670a76d5/6aa13f3e0a3bef09c363535c/Data_security(1800)_ber1a_Alamy.png?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: ber1a via Alamy Stock Photo

[](https://www.linkedin.com/sharing/share-offsite/?url=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/matters-india-cybersecurity-pre-series-ai-data-risks)[](http://www.facebook.com/sharer/sharer.php?u=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/matters-india-cybersecurity-pre-series-ai-data-risks)[](http://www.twitter.com/intent/tweet?url=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/matters-india-cybersecurity-pre-series-ai-data-risks)[](https://www.reddit.com/submit?url=https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/matters-india-cybersecurity-pre-series-ai-data-risks&title=Matters.AI%20Lands%20India%E2%80%99s%20Largest%20Cybersecurity%20Pre-Series%20A%20Round%20to%20Fight%20AI%20Data%20Risks)[](https://bsky.app/intent/compose?text=Matters.AI%20Lands%20India%E2%80%99s%20Largest%20Cybersecurity%20Pre-Series%20A%20Round%20to%20Fight%20AI%20Data%20Risks%20-%20https%3A%2F%2Fwww.darkreading.com%2Fcyber-risk%2Fmatters-india-cybersecurity-pre-series-ai-data-risks)[](mailto:?subject=Matters.AI%20Lands%20India%E2%80%99s%20Largest%20Cybersecurity%20Pre-Series%20A%20Round%20to%20Fight%20AI%20Data%20Risks&body=I%20thought%20the%20following%20from%20Dark%20Reading%20might%20interest%20you.%0D%0A%0D%0A%20Matters.AI%20Lands%20India%E2%80%99s%20Largest%20Cybersecurity%20Pre-Series%20A%20Round%20to%20Fight%20AI%20Data%20Risks%0D%0Ahttps%3A%2F%2Fwww.darkreading.com%2Fcyber-risk%2Fmatters-india-cybersecurity-pre-series-ai-data-risks)

As companies rapidly adopt AI, autonomous AI agents are increasingly accessing, copying, and moving sensitive business data using standard employee permissions. Traditional security tools show defenders where the organization's data is stored, but cannot differentiate between a human or agent accessing it, or what is happening to that data. Cybersecurity teams are forced to manually cobble together a view based on conflicting alerts from multiple tools.This is the problem Indian security startup Matters.AI is tacking with its data detection and response platform. Security teams can use Matters.AI's platform to gain visibility over cloud and on-premises systems, SaaS applications, endpoints, and browsers and use artificial intelligence to investigate and stop threats. The company just announced a $12 million pre-series A round (₹115 crore), bringing its total funding to $18.25 million (₹175 crore). This funding round is India's largest cybersecurity pre-Series A funding round, the company said.

Related:[It's a Bot World. We're Just Living in It](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/bot-world-just-living)

According to the company, Matters.AI combines four separate legacy security tools—data security posture management, activity monitoring, insider risk, and data loss prevention—into a single system to find and classify data; track movement of data across enterprise systems; and detect and fix issues. AI is used to find and organize the organization's sensitive data and then follow them as they get copied or shared. The platform combines data sensitivity and user access, and infers user intent to flag potential compliance risks and insider threats.

Matters.AI says the new funding will be used to drive research into automated investigations, endpoint intelligence, and safeguards against AI agents misusing data.

# L o a d i n g...

The company was co-founded by Keshava Murthy (CEO) and Harsh Sahu (CTO). Sahu was a founding machine learning team member at Observe.AI and brings AI/ML expertise to Matters.AI. This founding round was led by Tanglin Venture Partners, alongside IIFL Fintech Fund, Piper Serica's Bharat Tech Fund, Foster Ventures, and Tetration Ventures, with continued backing from Kalaari Capital and Endiya Partners. Endiya Partners has a deep information security portfolio, including threat intelligence company CloudSEK, Scrut Automation, ShieldSquare (acquired by Radware in 2019), and Shamrock AI. Kalaari Capital has backed NudgeBee and byteXL.

[21] SecurityWeek - In Other News: Tanker Cyberattacks, EU Quantum Act, New VirusTotal Capabilities
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 11:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/in-other-news-tanker-cyberattacks-eu-quantum-act-new-virustotal-capabilities/

[TAM METİN - 15759 kelime]
In Other News: Tanker Cyberattacks, EU Quantum Act, New VirusTotal Capabilities

Noteworthy stories that might have slipped under the radar: RansomwareHelp accused of profiting from ransom payments, USDA payment systems breach, cybersecurity firm Radware hacked.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

SecurityWeek’s weekly cybersecurity news roundup offers a concise overview of important developments that may not receive full standalone coverage yet remain relevant to the broader threat landscape.
This curated summary highlights key stories across vulnerability disclosures, emerging attack methods, policy updates, industry reports, and other noteworthy events to help readers stay well-informed about the evolving cybersecurity environment.



Here are this week’s highlights: 
ChatGPT Sites exposure
Pluto researchers combed web archives, public code repositories, search engines and other sources and found 16,666 sites created with ChatGPT Sites (which builds and hosts web apps for ChatGPT and Codex users), 12,830 of which were accessible to anyone. Manual checks confirmed sensitive data exposure at 27 organizations, including a Fortune 500 company’s sales dashboard, a freight forwarder’s customer quotes, and a vendor’s sales pitch. Sites are visible only to their owners and admins unless shared, but owners can open them to the entire internet.



Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


RansomwareHelp co-founder accused of profiting from ransom payments
US authorities have charged Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, a Colombian national and co-founder of the ransomware recovery firm RansomwareHelp, with three counts of wire fraud. Prosecutors allege that instead of using its own decryption technology as advertised, his company purchased decryptors from cybercriminals at prices significantly lower than what it charged customers. In one case involving undercover FBI agents, Palacio allegedly paid a $6,000 ransom and billed the purported victim $13,260 for decrypting their files.



USDA payment systems breach
A system used by the US Department of Agriculture (USDA) to make payments to farmers has been breached, according to NBC News. Two former department officials said the intrusion could let hackers access databases containing sensitive information on food safety and diseases carried by animals.



SolarWinds and Adobe patch vulnerabilities
SolarWinds has patched several critical vulnerabilities in its Serv-U managed file transfer product, including flaws that can lead to privilege escalation and arbitrary code execution. Adobe has addressed critical security holes in Campaign Classic and Experience Manager, including ones that allow arbitrary code execution, and a medium-severity DoS vulnerability in InDesign.



Container Store restores systems after cyberattack
US retailer The Container Store has restored its systems following a cyberattack that prevented customers from placing orders online. There is currently no evidence that customers’ financial information was stolen. The n0n ransomware group listed the company on its leak site earlier this month, but The Container Store has not addressed the claim.



VirusTotal adds new ransomware detection capabilities
VirusTotal has introduced new ransomware detection capabilities that rely on evidence of user files being encrypted during sandbox analysis. The improvements include a new behavior tag and stricter requirements for assigning the MITRE ATT&CK technique associated with data encryption for impact. VirusTotal demonstrated the effectiveness of the changes against ransomware that evaded detection by all 75 static antivirus engines at the time of analysis.



$17 billion targeted in US government anti-scam operation
The US Justice Department’s crackdown on fraud has targeted more than 800 defendants across over 400 cases since March. Authorities have also helped disrupt scam centers in more than 10 countries. According to the FBI, its Operation Blackout has led to the seizure or restraint of nearly $17 billion.



McDonald’s Japan hacked
McDonald’s Japan has disclosed a breach involving a database storing information associated with its mobile application. Hackers gained access to customer purchase histories, device location information, internal customer identifiers and IP addresses, but not names, contact details or credit card data. The number of impacted customers has not been disclosed.



Bithumb uncovers wider breach
South Korean cryptocurrency exchange Bithumb says more individuals and more types of personal information were compromised in an October cyberattack than initially reported. Bithumb also said the hackers had access for more than two weeks, not one hour as initially believed. The hackers stole data from an internal collaboration system, including identity documents, bank account numbers, transaction histories, and crypto wallet addresses. South Korean media reported that the initial victim count exceeded 7,000, including customers and staff. 



Artex gains traction among state-sponsored hackers
Microsoft has observed several state-sponsored hacking groups, including China’s Salt Typhoon, as well as cybercriminals using the AI-powered penetration testing framework Artex. Hackers have leveraged the tool to identify exposed secrets in public code repositories, conduct supply chain attacks and find vulnerable systems. Microsoft says the framework can significantly accelerate intrusions, with attackers achieving privileged access and establishing persistence within minutes.



Quantum Act targets Europe’s cryptographic readiness
The European Commission’s proposed Quantum Act includes measures to accelerate the development and adoption of post-quantum cryptography and prepare the EU for the security risks posed by quantum computers. The legislation is part of a broader effort to strengthen Europe’s quantum technology industry, reduce reliance on foreign suppliers and establish global leadership in the field by 2030.



Tanker cyberattacks 
Up to 50 ships carrying oil, liquefied petroleum, chemicals and other potentially dangerous cargo are believed to have been targeted in a wave of cyberattacks, according to The Wall Street Journal. US authorities are investigating whether Iran is responsible for the attacks.



Radware hacked
An internal report obtained by BleepingComputer shows that hackers breached cybersecurity firm Radware through an unprotected Cloud WAF management API exposed to the internet. Customer contact information and credentials associated with AWS, Azure and SFTP storage used by some customers may have been compromised. The company has not responded to SecurityWeek’s request for comment. 



Related: In Other News: New CISA Director Nominee, Connected Car Hacking, Baker Tilly Data Breach
Related: In Other News: Norwegian Energy Firm Cyberattacks, Supercomputer Hack, New Nightmare Eclipse Exploit

Up to 50 ships carrying oil, liquefied petroleum, chemicals and other potentially dangerous cargo are believed to have been targeted in a wave of cyberattacks, according to The Wall Street Journal. US authorities are investigating whether Iran is responsible for the attacks.



Radware hacked
An internal report obtained by BleepingComputer shows that hackers breached cybersecurity firm Radware through an unprotected Cloud WAF management API exposed to the internet. Customer contact information and credentials associated with AWS, Azure and SFTP storage used by some customers may have been compromised. The company has not responded to SecurityWeek’s request for comment. 



Related: In Other News: New CISA Director Nominee, Connected Car Hacking, Baker Tilly Data Breach
Related: In Other News: Norwegian Energy Firm Cyberattacks, Supercomputer Hack, New Nightmare Eclipse Exploit

ChatGPT Sites exposure
Pluto researchers combed web archives, public code repositories, search engines and other sources and found 16,666 sites created with ChatGPT Sites (which builds and hosts web apps for ChatGPT and Codex users), 12,830 of which were accessible to anyone. Manual checks confirmed sensitive data exposure at 27 organizations, including a Fortune 500 company’s sales dashboard, a freight forwarder’s customer quotes, and a vendor’s sales pitch. Sites are visible only to their owners and admins unless shared, but owners can open them to the entire internet.



Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


RansomwareHelp co-founder accused of profiting from ransom payments
US authorities have charged Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, a Colombian national and co-founder of the ransomware recovery firm RansomwareHelp, with three counts of wire fraud. Prosecutors allege that instead of using its own decryption technology as advertised, his company purchased decryptors from cybercriminals at prices significantly lower than what it charged customers. In one case involving undercover FBI agents, Palacio allegedly paid a $6,000 ransom and billed the purported victim $13,260 for decrypting their files.



USDA payment systems breach
A system used by the US Department of Agriculture (USDA) to make payments to farmers has been breached, according to NBC News. Two former department officials said the intrusion could let hackers access databases containing sensitive information on food safety and diseases carried by animals.



SolarWinds and Adobe patch vulnerabilities
SolarWinds has patched several critical vulnerabilities in its Serv-U managed file transfer product, including flaws that can lead to privilege escalation and arbitrary code execution. Adobe has addressed critical security holes in Campaign Classic and Experience Manager, including ones that allow arbitrary code execution, and a medium-severity DoS vulnerability in InDesign.



Container Store restores systems after cyberattack
US retailer The Container Store has restored its systems following a cyberattack that prevented customers from placing orders online. There is currently no evidence that customers’ financial information was stolen. The n0n ransomware group listed the company on its leak site earlier this month, but The Container Store has not addressed the claim.



VirusTotal adds new ransomware detection capabilities
VirusTotal has introduced new ransomware detection capabilities that rely on evidence of user files being encrypted during sandbox analysis. The improvements include a new behavior tag and stricter requirements for assigning the MITRE ATT&CK technique associated with data encryption for impact. VirusTotal demonstrated the effectiveness of the changes against ransomware that evaded detection by all 75 static antivirus engines at the time of analysis.



$17 billion targeted in US government anti-scam operation
The US Justice Department’s crackdown on fraud has targeted more than 800 defendants across over 400 cases since March. Authorities have also helped disrupt scam centers in more than 10 countries. According to the FBI, its Operation Blackout has led to the seizure or restraint of nearly $17 billion.



McDonald’s Japan hacked
McDonald’s Japan has disclosed a breach involving a database storing information associated with its mobile application. Hackers gained access to customer purchase histories, device location information, internal customer identifiers and IP addresses, but not names, contact details or credit card data. The number of impacted customers has not been disclosed.



Bithumb uncovers wider breach
South Korean cryptocurrency exchange Bithumb says more individuals and more types of personal information were compromised in an October cyberattack than initially reported. Bithumb also said the hackers had access for more than two weeks, not one hour as initially believed. The hackers stole data from an internal collaboration system, including identity documents, bank account numbers, transaction histories, and crypto wallet addresses. South Korean media reported that the initial victim count exceeded 7,000, including customers and staff. 



Artex gains traction among state-sponsored hackers
Microsoft has observed several state-sponsored hacking groups, including China’s Salt Typhoon, as well as cybercriminals using the AI-powered penetration testing framework Artex. Hackers have leveraged the tool to identify exposed secrets in public code repositories, conduct supply chain attacks and find vulnerable systems. Microsoft says the framework can significantly accelerate intrusions, with attackers achieving privileged access and establishing persistence within minutes.



Quantum Act targets Europe’s cryptographic readiness
The European Commission’s proposed Quantum Act includes measures to accelerate the development and adoption of post-quantum cryptography and prepare the EU for the security risks posed by quantum computers. The legislation is part of a broader effort to strengthen Europe’s quantum technology industry, reduce reliance on foreign suppliers and establish global leadership in the field by 2030.

Up to 50 ships carrying oil, liquefied petroleum, chemicals and other potentially dangerous cargo are believed to have been targeted in a wave of cyberattacks, according to The Wall Street Journal. US authorities are investigating whether Iran is responsible for the attacks.

The company has not responded to SecurityWeek’s request for comment.

The US Justice Department’s crackdown on fraud has targeted more than 800 defendants across over 400 cases since March. Authorities have also helped disrupt scam centers in more than 10 countries. According to the FBI, its Operation Blackout has led to the seizure or restraint of nearly $17 billion.



McDonald’s Japan hacked
McDonald’s Japan has disclosed a breach involving a database storing information associated with its mobile application. Hackers gained access to customer purchase histories, device location information, internal customer identifiers and IP addresses, but not names, contact details or credit card data. The number of impacted customers has not been disclosed.



Bithumb uncovers wider breach
South Korean cryptocurrency exchange Bithumb says more individuals and more types of personal information were compromised in an October cyberattack than initially reported. Bithumb also said the hackers had access for more than two weeks, not one hour as initially believed. The hackers stole data from an internal collaboration system, including identity documents, bank account numbers, transaction histories, and crypto wallet addresses. South Korean media reported that the initial victim count exceeded 7,000, including customers and staff. 



Artex gains traction among state-sponsored hackers
Microsoft has observed several state-sponsored hacking groups, including China’s Salt Typhoon, as well as cybercriminals using the AI-powered penetration testing framework Artex. Hackers have leveraged the tool to identify exposed secrets in public code repositories, conduct supply chain attacks and find vulnerable systems. Microsoft says the framework can significantly accelerate intrusions, with attackers achieving privileged access and establishing persistence within minutes.



Quantum Act targets Europe’s cryptographic readiness
The European Commission’s proposed Quantum Act includes measures to accelerate the development and adoption of post-quantum cryptography and prepare the EU for the security risks posed by quantum computers. The legislation is part of a broader effort to strengthen Europe’s quantum technology industry, reduce reliance on foreign suppliers and establish global leadership in the field by 2030.



Tanker cyberattacks 
Up to 50 ships carrying oil, liquefied petroleum, chemicals and other potentially dangerous cargo are believed to have been targeted in a wave of cyberattacks, according to The Wall Street Journal. US authorities are investigating whether Iran is responsible for the attacks.



Radware hacked
An internal report obtained by BleepingComputer shows that hackers breached cybersecurity firm Radware through an unprotected Cloud WAF management API exposed to the internet. Customer contact information and credentials associated with AWS, Azure and SFTP storage used by some customers may have been compromised. The company has not responded to SecurityWeek’s request for comment. 



Related: In Other News: New CISA Director Nominee, Connected Car Hacking, Baker Tilly Data Breach
Related: In Other News: Norwegian Energy Firm Cyberattacks, Supercomputer Hack, New Nightmare Eclipse Exploit

SolarWinds has patched several critical vulnerabilities in its Serv-U managed file transfer product, including flaws that can lead to privilege escalation and arbitrary code execution. Adobe has addressed critical security holes in Campaign Classic and Experience Manager, including ones that allow arbitrary code execution, and a medium-severity DoS vulnerability in InDesign.



Container Store restores systems after cyberattack
US retailer The Container Store has restored its systems following a cyberattack that prevented customers from placing orders online. There is currently no evidence that customers’ financial information was stolen. The n0n ransomware group listed the company on its leak site earlier this month, but The Container Store has not addressed the claim.



VirusTotal adds new ransomware detection capabilities
VirusTotal has introduced new ransomware detection capabilities that rely on evidence of user files being encrypted during sandbox analysis. The improvements include a new behavior tag and stricter requirements for assigning the MITRE ATT&CK technique associated with data encryption for impact. VirusTotal demonstrated the effectiveness of the changes against ransomware that evaded detection by all 75 static antivirus engines at the time of analysis.

Tanker cyberattacks 
Up to 50 ships carrying oil, liquefied petroleum, chemicals and other potentially dangerous cargo are believed to have been targeted in a wave of cyberattacks, according to The Wall Street Journal. US authorities are investigating whether Iran is responsible for the attacks.



Radware hacked
An internal report obtained by BleepingComputer shows that hackers breached cybersecurity firm Radware through an unprotected Cloud WAF management API exposed to the internet. Customer contact information and credentials associated with AWS, Azure and SFTP storage used by some customers may have been compromised. The company has not responded to SecurityWeek’s request for comment.

Microsoft has observed several state-sponsored hacking groups, including China’s Salt Typhoon, as well as cybercriminals using the AI-powered penetration testing framework Artex. Hackers have leveraged the tool to identify exposed secrets in public code repositories, conduct supply chain attacks and find vulnerable systems. Microsoft says the framework can significantly accelerate intrusions, with attackers achieving privileged access and establishing persistence within minutes.

[22] SecurityWeek - 100,000 Potentially Impacted by Colorado Data Breach
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 09:30:54 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/100000-potentially-impacted-by-colorado-data-breach/

[TAM METİN - 2453 kelime]
100,000 Potentially Impacted by Colorado Data Breach

Hackers breached the digital identification and document storage application myColorado and accessed user-uploaded data.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

The Colorado Governor’s Office of Information Technology (OIT) has disclosed a data breach impacting data that residents uploaded to the myColorado application.
Colorado’s official mobile application, myColorado is used for digital identification, state services, and document storage. It has approximately 2.1 million users.



OIT took the application offline last week, after identifying unauthorized access to platform data, and immediately initiated incident response and an investigation into the incident.



One week after the outage began, OIT said it discovered the incident after the attackers triggered the automated threat detection systems deployed within its Amazon Web Services (AWS) environment.



The investigation has determined that the attackers accessed documents stored within the ‘myCards and Documents’ section of the application, which includes user-uploaded data, such as PDF files and photos of government IDs, vehicle registrations, and insurance cards.



According to Colorado, approximately 100,000 individuals might have been affected, but no payment information was accessed.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Staff are working around the clock to match the inappropriately accessed documents to individual users. As that matching continues, OIT will further refine estimates on the number of people whose data was involved,” Colorado said.



The myColorado application remains offline and OIT has not announced a restoration date. The state expects to start sending direct notifications to the affected individuals on November 27.



“Our main goal is to protect the security of users. Additionally, we are prioritizing direct notification of users involved in the incident and getting the app up and running as soon as possible, with security enhancements in place,” said state CIO and OIT executive director Sarah Tuneberg.



Related: Hex Package Manager Warns of Potential Data Breach
Related: SAP Confirms Cyberattack, Source Code Reportedly Compromised
Related: FIFA Says It Has Been Victim of a Cyberattack Accessing ‘Personal and Business Information’
Related: 1Password Breach Exposes Customer Data, Internal Source Code

[23] SecurityWeek - Two Newly Disclosed GeoServer Vulnerabilities Under Attack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 15:27:36 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/two-newly-disclosed-geoserver-vulnerabilities-under-attack/

[TAM METİN - 3325 kelime]
Two Newly Disclosed GeoServer Vulnerabilities Under Attack

Attackers are probing GeoServer instances for two high-severity flaws that can be chained with other bugs for unauthenticated RCE.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Threat actors are targeting two recently disclosed GeoServer vulnerabilities that can be chained with other flaws to achieve unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE).
Cybersecurity firm WatchTowr has observed attempts to exploit two high-severity GeoServer vulnerabilities tracked as CVE-2026-120705 and CVE-2026-129243. The activity appears to be aimed at identifying vulnerable servers.



“Attackers sent error-based exploit probes to validate that the server is vulnerable by creating and invoking stored queries,” WatchTowr noted.



WatchTowr has not reported any instances of attackers successfully achieving remote code execution. At the time of writing there do not appear to be any other reports of exploitation attempts.



GeoServer is an open source application for publishing and sharing geospatial data. Government agencies widely use it, and utilities also deploy it. Cybersecurity company Eye Security has identified more than 3,000 internet-accessible GeoServer instances.



CVE-2026-120705 is an XML injection vulnerability that can allow an unauthenticated attacker to obtain sensitive files or conduct server-side request forgery (SSRF) attacks. CVE-2026-129243 can enable attackers to bypass access controls and perform operations typically reserved for administrators.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The two security holes are part of a five-bug exploit chain discovered by Rapid7 researcher Jonah Burgess, aka CryptoCat, who reported his findings to GeoServer developers in early August. 



Burgess also reported CVE-2026-129244, CVE-2026-129245, and CVE-2026-129242. He showed how an attacker can gain admin access and execute arbitrary commands with the GeoServer process’s privileges.



The researcher disclosed technical details this week and published PoC exploit code. 



Eye Security has independently found some of the vulnerabilities. It demonstrated unauthenticated RCE on Linux and Windows by combining three weaknesses: CVE-2026-120705, CVE-2026-129245, and CVE-2026-120704. The security firm said it reported its findings to the vendor in late September. 



GeoServer released patches several weeks ago, and it published advisories in recent days. Organizations are advised to upgrade to GeoServer 3.0.2, 2.28.6, or 2.27.7.



GeoServer vulnerabilities have been targeted in the past. In August 2026, hackers exploited a vulnerability as a zero-day, and in 2025 it came to light that a US government agency had been breached through the exploitation of a GeoServer flaw.



Related: Drupal Flaw Found by Anthropic Scanner Exploited in Attacks
Related: Hackers Exploit Unpatched Flaw in Defunct Idunno AntiCsrf Library
Related: Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls

WatchTowr has not reported any instances of attackers successfully achieving remote code execution. At the time of writing there do not appear to be any other reports of exploitation attempts.



GeoServer is an open source application for publishing and sharing geospatial data. Government agencies widely use it, and utilities also deploy it. Cybersecurity company Eye Security has identified more than 3,000 internet-accessible GeoServer instances.



CVE-2026-120705 is an XML injection vulnerability that can allow an unauthenticated attacker to obtain sensitive files or conduct server-side request forgery (SSRF) attacks. CVE-2026-129243 can enable attackers to bypass access controls and perform operations typically reserved for administrators.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The two security holes are part of a five-bug exploit chain discovered by Rapid7 researcher Jonah Burgess, aka CryptoCat, who reported his findings to GeoServer developers in early August. 



Burgess also reported CVE-2026-129244, CVE-2026-129245, and CVE-2026-129242. He showed how an attacker can gain admin access and execute arbitrary commands with the GeoServer process’s privileges.



The researcher disclosed technical details this week and published PoC exploit code. 



Eye Security has independently found some of the vulnerabilities. It demonstrated unauthenticated RCE on Linux and Windows by combining three weaknesses: CVE-2026-120705, CVE-2026-129245, and CVE-2026-120704. The security firm said it reported its findings to the vendor in late September. 



GeoServer released patches several weeks ago, and it published advisories in recent days. Organizations are advised to upgrade to GeoServer 3.0.2, 2.28.6, or 2.27.7.



GeoServer vulnerabilities have been targeted in the past. In August 2026, hackers exploited a vulnerability as a zero-day, and in 2025 it came to light that a US government agency had been breached through the exploitation of a GeoServer flaw.

[24] Help Net Security - FIFA hacked, cybercriminals claim access to sensitive internal records
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 13:21:50 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/fifa-cyberattack-data-leak/

[TAM METİN - 319 kelime]
FIFA hacked, cybercriminals claim access to sensitive internal records

Cybercriminals broke into FIFA’s systems and gained access to “a limited set of personal and business information,” world football’s governing body confirmed on Thursday.

“FIFA is working closely with the relevant authorities, including law enforcement,” the Zurich-based football governing body told The Associated Press, without saying when the attack happened or what kind of information the intruders took.

“FIFA condemns in the strongest possible terms the unlawful acquisition and publication of information, particularly personal data. Such actions constitute a serious and unacceptable violation of privacy and can have significant consequences for the individuals concerned,” a FIFA spokesperson added.

The confirmation came a week after a group calling itself the Handsome Boy Hacking Club (HBHC) posted a screenshot on X of what it said were FIFA files.

According to the group, the data was exported from FIFA’s Microsoft environment, and the haul contains “a full export from their internal governance systems, as well as some other assorted documents and screenshots from various services.”

The post lists identity documents, immigration records, executive information, financial data and legal documents among the stolen material.

HBHC is a relatively new cybercrime group that listed its first victim on its data leak site on November 19.

“We would like to clarify that we are purely financially motivated. We are not interested in the country of origin,” the gang wrote, before signing off with a promise of “more leaks soon.“

The attack couldn’t have come at a worse time for FIFA president Gianni Infantino, who is under scrutiny after a scrapped plan to sell stakes in the World Cup to private investors.

FIFA is the second football organization to disclose a cyberattack this week. On Monday, Spain’s LaLiga confirmed that a cybercriminal broke into one of its fan services platforms and stole personal data of people registered with the league’s digital services and those of some clubs.

[25] Help Net Security - Chinese and North Korean state hackers add ARTEX AI framework to their arsenal
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 11:06:57 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/27/artex-ai-pentest-framework-china-north-korea-state-hackers/

[TAM METİN - 525 kelime]
Chinese and North Korean state hackers add ARTEX AI framework to their arsenal

State-sponsored hacking groups tied to Beijing and Pyongyang have started running the ARTEX pentesting tool in attacks, Microsoft Threat Intelligence warned this week.

Published on GitHub in July 2026 by a user going by Autumn-27, ARTEX is an LLM-driven open-source penetration testing framework that runs attacks largely on its own, with the operator setting a goal and AI agents planning and carrying out the steps needed to reach it.

In November, China-based cybersecurity company ThreatBook claimed ownership of ARTEX and began selling access to an ARTEX Pro version, though Microsoft does not link the company to the attacks.

“In the past few weeks, we’ve reported several distinct campaigns that abused ARTEX to search public repositories for exposed secrets, enable supply chain compromise, and scan internet-facing systems for exploitation,” researchers wrote. “These campaigns illustrate how threat actors use ARTEX to automate various stages of the attack chain.”

“As of November 2026, Microsoft Threat Intelligence has observed both state-sponsored and financially motivated threat actors such as Storm-0940, Storm-3358, Onyx Sleet, Pearl Sleet, Crystal Typhoon, and Salt Typhoon integrating ARTEX into their tooling,” Microsoft reported.

Typhoon is the label Microsoft uses for China-based groups, while Sleet marks those tied to North Korea.

The most recognizable group on the list, Salt Typhoon, is linked to a long-running espionage campaign against telecommunications providers.

Salt Typhoon was also tied to a July 2025 intrusion at a European telecommunications organization. The attackers got in by exploiting a vulnerability in a Citrix NetScaler Gateway appliance and were spotted before they could move deeper into the network, Darktrace reported.

Three hours to privileged access

In one password spraying run against Microsoft 365, Storm-0940, a China-state-aligned group, logged more than 151,000 failed sign-ins, peaking at 2,900 failures per minute. The group went from initial targeting to privileged access in less than three hours.

Another case moved faster, with an AI agent going from searching GitHub for client secrets to taking data from Azure Key Vault in five minutes.

Storm-0940’s ARTEX-assisted campaigns also include a supply chain compromise linked to ConnectWise and the abuse of app credentials.

“ARTEX does not introduce new attack techniques; it uses common, pre-existing penetration testing tools and techniques. ARTEX does sometimes transmit distinctive characteristics associated with its integrated penetration testing tools. However, threat actors using ARTEX can and do vary network indicators like source IP address or user agent string, especially to bypass static or simple signature-based detection and blocking,” Microsoft explained.

Behavior is harder to disguise, and by default ARTEX makes no attempt to hide what it does. Its bursts of varied requests, rapid retries and jumps between attack stages at machine speed stand out in logs.

The UK’s National Cyber Security Centre came to the same conclusion about agentic attack frameworks in general. In a blog post published on November 24, the agency said such tools “trade stealth for speed,” generating heavy traffic and interacting with almost everything they find, which makes them easier to detect. It also warned that this may change once future frameworks are built with stealth in mind.

National Cyber Security Centre

[26] The Record - Japan’s education ministry discloses breach of government procurement system
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 14:15:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/japan-education-ministry-cyberattack

[TAM METİN - 444 kelime]
Japan’s education ministry discloses breach of government procurement system

Japan’s education ministry disclosed earlier this week that hackers had breached its government procurement system, potentially exposing records containing business information and email addresses.

The Ministry of Education, Culture, Sports, Science and Technology (MEXT) said it detected the intrusion on October 1, nearly two months before publicly disclosing the incident. An investigation found that hackers may have obtained information stored in the system’s database.

The potentially compromised data includes nearly 1,700 email addresses and 164,000 records containing company names, the names of employees and other business information, the ministry said in a statement Wednesday.

The affected system is used to publish information about government tenders, contracts and procurement opportunities. The potentially exposed business information relates to companies eligible to participate in the ministry’s bidding processes.

The ministry said it had blocked all communications between the affected system and internal and external networks and found no evidence that the potentially compromised information has been publicly disclosed or misused.

The incident was reported to Japan’s data protection authorities, and the ministry is continuing its investigation in coordination with relevant government agencies. It has not publicly identified the perpetrators or disclosed how they gained access to its systems.

“We will continue to investigate the incident in close cooperation with the relevant authorities and take appropriate action,” MEXT said.

This is the latest cybersecurity incident affecting major Japanese companies and public institutions in recent months.

Earlier this week, Japanese railway operator Keio Corporation said a ransomware attack in September may have compromised the personal information of more than 11 million customers, business partners, employees and job applicants. The ransomware group Qilin, believed to have Russian origins, claimed responsibility for the attack.

McDonald’s Japan also recently disclosed a breach involving a database linked to its official mobile app. The compromised information included customers’ purchase histories, IP addresses, internal customer identification numbers and mobile device location data.

Earlier this month, the city of Osaka disclosed that a breach involving a third-party technology provider had exposed more than 1 million email addresses associated with an online platform for municipal services.

Other major Japanese companies that have reported cybersecurity incidents in recent months include telecommunications giant KDDI, the Japanese unit of U.S. insurer Aflac, electronics manufacturer Nidec and brewer Sapporo Holdings. There is no indication that these incidents are connected.

is a reporter for Recorded Future News based in Ukraine. She writes about cybersecurity startups, cyberattacks in Eastern Europe and the state of the cyberwar between Ukraine and Russia. She previously was a tech reporter for Forbes Ukraine. Her work has also been published at Sifted, The Kyiv Independent and The Kyiv Post.

[27] The Record - Software giant SAP confirms cyber incident amid reports of source code breach
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 13:15:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/sap-germany-cyberattack-github

[TAM METİN - 449 kelime]
Software giant SAP confirms cyber incident amid reports of source code breach

German software giant SAP confirmed a cybersecurity incident following reports that hackers infiltrated its software development infrastructure, potentially gaining access to the company’s source code.

In a statement to Recorded Future News on Friday, SAP said it had identified suspicious activity and immediately taken steps to protect its customers and secure its services. The company did not disclose the nature or extent of the intrusion.

“Based on our investigation to date, we have found no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data,” SAP said. “We will continue to strengthen our security to provide the secure and resilient environment our customers depend on.”

German magazine Der Spiegel first reported the incident Thursday, citing internal company communications and sources familiar with the matter.

According to the magazine, hackers targeted SAP’s software development environment hosted on GitHub, obtaining employee authentication tokens that  allowed them to copy and modify the company’s source code.

The German Press Agency (dpa), which independently reviewed portions of internal SAP communication, reported that the company had alerted employees to suspicious activity within its GitHub environment several days before the incident became public. Employees were advised to assess whether they needed to change their login credentials or take additional security precautions.

SAP launched an internal incident response operation code-named “Project Granite,” according to German business newspaper Handelsblatt.

The company’s emergency security measures reportedly affected several software development teams. SAP restricted access to GitHub, halted automated development processes and replaced digital keys used to verify that its software was genuine and had not been tampered with.

It remains unclear whether the hackers accessed SAP’s proprietary source code or whether any altered code ended up in software delivered to customers.

SAP has not publicly identified who was behind the attack, how the hackers gained access to its systems or whether the incident has been fully contained.

SAP is one of the world’s largest enterprise software companies whose products are used by businesses and public institutions worldwide to manage critical operations, including accounting, procurement, supply chains and human resources.

The incident follows a separate software supply chain attack in April, when hackers injected malicious code into several open-source software packages developed by SAP in an attempt to steal sensitive information from developers and automated software development environments. It is unclear whether the two incidents are connected.

[28] The Record - Cyberattack disrupts global news distribution platform at France’s AFP
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/france-afp-hit-by-cyberattack

[TAM METİN - 327 kelime]
Cyberattack disrupts global news distribution platform at France’s AFP

French international news agency Agence France-Presse (AFP) was hit by a cyberattack Thursday, forcing it to take offline one of its main platforms for distributing news content to media organizations worldwide.

The attack targeted AFP Forum, an online platform that news outlets use to browse and download the agency’s stories, photographs and videos.

AFP confirmed the incident Thursday in an internal email to employees, which was quoted in the agency’s own reporting. The agency is investigating the attack with assistance from external cybersecurity experts.

“AFP was hit by a cyber attack overnight. The target was AFP Forum, one of the platforms our clients use to browse and download our content,” AFP management said in the email.

“As soon as the intrusion was detected, our teams moved immediately to contain the intrusion and took AFP Forum offline as a precautionary measure. AFP Forum remains unavailable until further notice.”

AFP Forum remained inaccessible as of Friday, displaying a maintenance notice in several languages. The agency has not identified who was behind the attack or provided a timeline for restoring the service.

It remains unclear whether the hackers gained access to sensitive information or whether other AFP systems were affected.

The agency did not respond to a request for comment.

AFP is one of the world’s three major international news agencies, alongside Reuters and the Associated Press. It traces its roots to Agence Havas, founded in 1835, and took its current name in 1944. It employs more than 1,800 journalists and has 210 bureaus worldwide.

This is not the first cyberattack to disrupt the agency’s content distribution services.

[29] Microsoft Security - Microsoft named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Email Security
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:15:00 +0000
Link: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/11/25/microsoft-named-a-leader-in-the-2026-gartner-magic-quadrant-for-email-security/

[TAM METİN - 1106 kelime]
We’re honored to share that Microsoft has once again been named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Email Security.

AI is changing the threat landscape and this is shifting how organizations think about their existing security strategy. In response, we’ve invested in two areas over the past year: using AI to protect people and the agents that now read their email and making protection measurable so customers can verify that it works.

AI is changing both sides of the inbox

The first area of investment starts with a simple reality: AI now reads email alongside people, attackers are using AI to target both, and defense must use AI to stay ahead. Picture an ordinary vendor email: a meeting recap, a few action items, and a friendly sign-off. Hidden in white text on a white background is prompt injection: an instruction telling an AI assistant to gather financial documents and send them to an outside address. The employee isn’t the target; their AI agent is. People can be persuaded by messages that exploit fear, trust, or urgency, but agents don’t need persuading. Once an agent acts on an instruction, pulling the message back won’t undo it. This is why Microsoft Defender stops malicious messages before delivery and keeps reevaluating delivered mail as new intelligence comes in.

This summer, we introduced prompt injection protection for email in Defender to detect and quarantine messages carrying hidden malicious AI instructions before they reach Microsoft Copilot or other agents. In Defender, analysts can also see what an agent would see: the ‘Show concealed text’ button on the ‘Email Entity’ page reveals hidden content in the email preview, so they can inspect the instructions an agent could encounter. These attacks don’t show up often, yet. Over a four-week timeframe, we caught almost 10,000 prompt injection attempts, around 330 a day, out of the billions of emails Defender scans every day. The hard part is spotting those few without tripping over legitimate mail, so we don’t lean on a single model. Heuristics clear out the bulk first. What’s left goes to a fine-tuned small language model, and only the messages that still look suspicious reach a large language model for the final call. Saving the heaviest analysis for the smallest set of messages is what keeps false positives low and business email moving.

AI is also reshaping the attacks aimed at people. The phishing emails that worry us most are the ones that look completely normal. No malicious link, no infected attachment, just a plausible request written with perfect grammar and the right context. Generative AI makes those messages cheap to produce and endlessly variable. To meet that shift, we rebuilt how Microsoft Defender makes decisions rather than simply adding another filter. That decision-making now operates at scale, screening 5.2 billion emails a day on average1. The redesigned detection stack combines multiple machine learning models and LLM-based classifiers, with an ensemble model weighing their outputs to determine the final verdict. It also uses natural language processing signals, including message topic, to judge what a message is asking for and not only what it contains. That helps Defender catch attacks more accurately.

While detection helps with the message in front of you, defenders also need to see how it connects to the rest of an attack. Microsoft Perception extends that help beyond the message, connecting an email investigation to the broader exposure and response picture. Blue team agents investigate threats and improve detections, red team agents identify paths to compromise, and green team agents prioritize actions to close security gaps, while people retain control of critical decisions. The Integrated Security Operations Center (ISOC) in Microsoft Defender, launched in September, provides a shared foundation for people and AI agents to investigate and respond to the full attack—not just an isolated email. By bringing security information and event management (SIEM) and extended detection and response (XDR) together, it connects email signals with endpoint, identity, cloud, and third-party data, giving AI-assisted security operations broader context.

Proof over promises: Making protection measurable

But using AI to defend only matters if customers can verify that it works. The second  investment area is one the whole industry has struggled with: how do you know your email security is actually working? Detection claims are easy to make and hard to verify, so we publish the data.

Since July 2025, we’ve released quarterly email security benchmarking reports built on real-world threats, not synthetic tests. They compare environments protected by Microsoft Defender alone with those that add a secure email gateway in front of it or an integrated cloud email security (ICES) layer behind it. We believe transparency strengthens the whole market, and an industry that measures itself in the open improves faster.

While benchmarking shows how protection performs across organizations, the Protection & Posture Insights report shows how it performs in yours. This on-demand, tenant-specific report helps security leaders understand which threats target their organization, how well Microsoft Defender stops them, and where configuration can be strengthened. Recent updates show URL clicks checked and malicious-site attempts blocked across email, Microsoft Teams, Office apps, and Microsoft Copilot, and also highlight allow lists and overrides that may be letting threats through. Together, benchmarking and tenant-specific insights give leaders evidence to evaluate protection and identify opportunities to strengthen it.

We’re grateful to our customers, partners, and the analyst community for their trust, and to the teams turning insight into protection. Our engineers start with customer problems, while our researchers draw on more than 165 trillion signals a day across Microsoft to anticipate emerging threats and build protections before real-world examples appear2. This recognition from Gartner is a milestone for us, not the finish line. Our focus remains on protecting people and agents and giving customers evidence they can use to evaluate their defenses.

Explore the latest Microsoft Defender email security benchmarking results.

Are you a regular user of Microsoft Defender for email security? Share your insights on Gartner Peer Insights™.

Gartner, Magic Quadrant for Email Security, By Max Taggett, Franz Hinner, Deepak Mishra, 23 November 2026

Gartner publications consist of the opinions of Gartner’s business and technology insights organization and should not be construed as statements of fact. [37] Schneier on Security - Recent Outages Have Economic Causes
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: 2026-11-27T12:02:23Z
Link: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/recent-outages-have-economic-causes.html

[TAM METİN - 607 kelime]
Recent Outages Have Economic Causes

This essay originally appeared in The Guardian. It’s an expanded version of the one I posted as “We Get the Infrastructure We Pay For.”

It’s been a bad week for our computerized infrastructure. We’ve seen outages at Microsoft, the FAA, a handful of banks, United Airlines, an unnamed classified NSA network, and Apple. People should be forgiven for suspecting a coordinated cyberattack, a common cause, or at least a conspiracy. But there is none.

The incidents are unrelated. They’re not the result of any cyberattack, coordinated or not. And, even more surprising, they don’t seem to be the result of AI. (We can’t say anything about the NSA; we’ll probably never know the details of that one.) It’s really just a series of unfortunate coincidences.

None of them should surprise, though. Our computerized infrastructure is both vulnerable and fragile.

Much of it is old and has been incrementally patched and upgraded over the decades. A surprising amount of it is maintained by hobbyists, without any recompense. There often aren’t good backup systems. And more and more of it is centralized, meaning that individual failures have cascading consequences across many different aspects of our lives and society.

A lot of our tech infrastructure just barely works. And when it fails, it can fail badly. It’s the very opposite of resilience.

There are a lot of technological reasons why this is true, but they are all downstream from the economic ones. The technologies we rely on are built and maintained within our economic system—and that’s where the core problems lie.

Security, safety, resilience, redundancy and recoverability are all essential parts of reliable technology, but they are all costs. More importantly, they are costs that don’t generate near-term profits.

Modern shareholder capitalism, and private equity in particular, prioritizes near-term profits over everything else. This squeezes all apparent inefficiencies—and, additionally, all other positive intangibles—out of a company in pursuit of those profits.

Market competition, essential for the forces that nudge companies to provide better services, is another perceived inefficiency. Monopolization removes it in pursuit of near-term profits.

From the perspective of a quarterly budget, spending on reliability measures is an inefficiency. They’re expenses that don’t generate revenue. They feel wasteful  until something bad happens.

But, if you’re lucky, you can push the costs of failure on to society, further increasing profits. So, we get stranded in airports when planes don’t fly, or have to deal with the fallout when we can’t withdraw money from our bank accounts.

The effects of those economic pressures are brittle systems, with single points of failure and cascading failure modes. They’re more susceptible to cyberattack—both human and AI-fueled—and more susceptible to random mistakes.

The more we allow unregulated capitalism to privatize gains while socializing risks, the more we should expect these sorts of failures.

Upgrading our computerized systems, increasing their reliability, removing single points of failure, and making them more resilient are all technical problems. But they won’t happen until we fix the political and economic ones.

We need to change the economic incentives that make it profitable to operate these systems just at the edge of functionality, and to push the costs of failure on to all of us. Fixing that requires regulation—with enforcement and meaningful fines—and stringent antitrust enforcement.

But while that’s all easy to say, it’s much harder to implement—especially in the US where monied interests have such a tight hold on the legislative agenda. Until this changes, we should expect lots more of these sorts of failures in the future.

Tags: cybersecurity, economics of security, resilience

Posted on November 27, 2026 at 7:02 AM   •
   8 Comments

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/recent-outages-have-economic-causes.html, schneier.com, 27.11.2026)

[38] The Cyber Express - CERT-In Flags High-Severity Vulnerability in DIGIT e-Governance Platform
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 27 Nov 2026 09:25:50 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/cert-in-vulnerability-in-digit-platform/

[TAM METİN - 582 kelime]
CERT-In Flags High-Severity Vulnerability in DIGIT e-Governance Platform

The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) has warned of a high-severity vulnerability in DIGIT, an open-source platform used to build digital public services across the country.

The SQL injection flaw could let attackers run unauthorized database commands without logging in. The alert comes as advances in AI models make software weaknesses easier for attackers to find and exploit.

CERT-In Advisory on the Vulnerability in DIGIT

CERT-In published vulnerability notes CIVN-2026-0570 on November 25, 2026, and rated it “HIGH” in severity. The issue affects DIGIT Core version 2.9 and has been assigned the identifier CVE-2026-136706.

According to the agency, the flaw comes from the way the platform handles user input. This vulnerability exists in the DIGIT Platform due to improper neutralization of user-supplied input while constructing SQL queries and execution in the affected user and access control service components.

An unauthenticated remote attacker could exploit this vulnerability by sending specially crafted input through the affected API endpoint to the targeted system,” CERT-In said.

What Attackers Could Do

If exploited, the vulnerability in DIGIT could allow a remote attacker to execute arbitrary SQL queries on a targeted system. CERT-In said this could lead to manipulation of application logic and unauthorized disclosure, modification or deletion of database records.

The note does not say whether attackers have already used the flaw. It is aimed at organizations, system administrators, and maintainers of the DIGIT platform. CERT-In credited security researcher Sarthak Chauhan with reporting the issue.

Fix Released in DIGIT Core v2.9.3

The eGov Foundation, which operates DIGIT, has fixed the flaw in DIGIT Core v2.9.3. According to CERT-In, the foundation has directed platforms running affected deployments to update to the latest version immediately. The release is available on the foundation’s GitHub page (github.com/egovernments/Digit-Core/releases/tag/v2.9.3).

Organizations with queries can contact CERT-In, which operates under the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology, at [email protected] or +91-11-22902657. The agency’s office is at Electronics Niketan, 6, CGO Complex, Lodhi Road, New Delhi – 110003.

Why the Vulnerability in DIGIT Matters

DIGIT, short for Digital Infrastructure for Governance, Inclusion & Transformation, was created by the eGov Foundation. Nandan Nilekani and Srikanth Nadhamuni set up the foundation in 2003 to use technology for digital public and municipal services. It is currently led by CEO Viraj Tyagi, a cofounder of fintech startup Finnable.

The platform offers reusable building blocks and shared data registries for building sector-specific digital public infrastructure (DPI). Local governments, civic bodies, ecosystem partners and NGOs use it to roll out large-scale public service applications such as water billing, digital health registries, business licensing systems and vaccination portals.

Because so many civic services rely on it, a successful breach of applications built on DIGIT could expose sensitive citizen data and disrupt essential services.

AI Models Raise the Stakes

The CERT-In warning comes at a time when rapid progress in frontier AI models has made it considerably easier for hackers to exploit flaws in software. Recent disruptions involving Microsoft, Apple, JPMorgan Chase and the US Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) have also drawn attention to the risks that vulnerabilities pose in interconnected technology systems.
